# ELSim: agent-ready product and engineering roadmap

Status: active implementation roadmap, 2026-10-10. The application foundation is being built in this repository.

## 1. Product goal

Build an original, open-source circuit simulator that runs in modern browsers and can later be packaged for Windows, macOS, and Linux. The first experience should be as quick to learn as CircuitJS/Falstad: place parts, connect wires, press Run, see current flow, interact with switches, and probe voltages. It should grow toward LTspice-style schematic capture, hierarchical sheets, imported IC macromodels, and serious waveform analysis.

The product must not promise universal LTspice compatibility. ELSim will import supported unencrypted files, preserve unsupported content when practical, and produce explicit diagnostics. It will not bundle LTspice binaries, proprietary symbols, or vendor model libraries without a verified redistribution license.

### Product principles

- One electrical source of truth: ngspice results drive plots and animations. A visual effect must never silently change circuit behavior.
- Beginner path first: useful defaults, examples, direct manipulation, readable explanations, and progressive disclosure of SPICE details.
- Local first: circuit files and simulations work without an account or hosted compute service.
- Portable projects: a documented, versioned format with project-owned dependencies and deterministic import/export.
- Reproducible results: record ngspice version, settings, generated netlist, and model hashes with each run.
- Original UI, symbol art, examples, and documentation. Feature parity is a goal; copying CircuitJS implementation or branded assets is not.

## 2. Proposed architecture and tools

| Area | Initial choice | Why / decision gate |
| --- | --- | --- |
| App | TypeScript, React, Vite | Mature browser tooling and component ecosystem. Pin versions after a dependency license audit. |
| Schematic editor | Custom scene graph rendered with SVG initially | Crisp symbols, accessible hit targets, simpler editing. Benchmark before considering Canvas/WebGL for very large sheets. |
| Workspace panels | React layout with dockable waveform panels | In-app windows are browser UI; C++ rendering is unnecessary. Pop-out browser windows can be later. |
| Waveforms | Canvas or WebGL plotting layer behind an internal trace API | Handle large vectors and zoom/pan; benchmark and choose a maintained permissively licensed library or implement a thin renderer. |
| Simulation | Pinned ngspice source compiled by Emscripten into WebAssembly | Run in a dedicated worker. Use a narrow adapter so the native build can later use libngspice. |
| Native app | Tauri or similar, decision after web MVP | Reuse the UI; compare native engine packaging and platform support before choosing. |
| Project format | Versioned JSON manifest in a ZIP container (`.elsim`) | Holds sheets, symbols, models, settings, and provenance. Specify schemas before widespread use. |
| Quality | Node built-in test runner initially, Playwright, ngspice golden circuits, license/SBOM tooling | Test connectivity, numeric results, import round trips, and releases rather than UI snapshots alone. |

The browser worker is not the same as using Emscripten pthreads. Begin with a single-threaded ngspice build inside one Web Worker. If ngspice requires pthreads for chosen features, test that separately; deployed pthreads require browser cross-origin isolation headers. Do not assume all native ngspice extensions, dynamic model loaders, file access, or callbacks work unchanged in WebAssembly.

### Core boundaries

1. **Document model:** project, sheets, symbol definitions, component instances, wires, junctions, net labels, hierarchical ports, model assets, simulation configurations, plots, and UI state.
2. **Connectivity/netlister:** deterministic graph construction, node naming, hierarchy flattening, model references, and source mapping from generated netlist lines back to schematic objects.
3. **Engine adapter:** `loadProjectNetlist`, `run`, `cancel`, `subscribeVectors`, `getDiagnostics`, `dispose`; browser and native implementations share result types.
4. **Results store:** immutable run metadata, typed vectors with units and axes, sampling/downsampling, and value lookup at an animation time cursor.
5. **Presentation:** editor, library, properties, help, plot panels, diagnostics, and animations consume document/results APIs, never engine internals.

### Component schema requirements

Each definition has a stable ID and version; electrical type (`R`, `C`, `L`, `D`, `Q`, `M`, `X`, etc.); ordered pins with names, directions, and SPICE order; parameter schema with units and defaults; netlist template or model reference; symbol geometry; and optional animation channels. Each instance has its own ID, reference designator, transform, editable values, pin-to-net connections derived from geometry, and provenance. Imported `.asy` pin order must come from `SpiceOrder`, not visual position. Unknown imported attributes should be retained in a namespaced extension field for future export.

Implementation slice: [componentDefinitions.ts](src/domain/componentDefinitions.ts) provides versioned stable definitions for built-in parts and content-hash-addressed definitions for imported `.SUBCKT` models, with SPICE electrical type, ordered terminals, property names/units/defaults, result-driven animation channels, model parameters, and library provenance; [component-definition-v1.schema.json](schemas/component-definition-v1.schema.json) defines the interchange contract. App placement reads defaults from definitions; component names, SPICE prefixes, and generic inspector labels/units derive from the built-in registry. Imported model cards expose their definition ID, ordered pins, model parameters, and source filename; placement and symbol matching consume the definition’s ordered pins. Each placed project part persists `componentDefinitionId` in schema v8. Validation backfills it for legacy projects, including v7 projects and nested sheets, derives imported subcircuit IDs from the model library content hash, and rejects IDs that do not match the instance’s built-in kind/model. LTspice imports and new app placements attach IDs directly. Domain coverage checks migration, imported identity, nested sheets, archive round trips, and mismatch rejection; all 21 test groups, typecheck, and production build pass.

Example animation bindings: lamp brightness from dissipated power with configurable nominal power; capacitor fill/color from voltage or stored energy; inductor effect from current; wire particles from current direction and magnitude; switch motion from its actual state. Clamp and explain all mappings. Animation sampling must be decoupled from solver time steps and must not suggest electrical precision it does not have.

## 3. Milestones and issue backlog

Every issue must include owner, dependencies, acceptance criteria, test evidence, documentation changes, and license impact. Agents may work in parallel only across explicit boundaries; avoid concurrent edits to the project schema or engine adapter without an agreed contract.

### M0 — Foundations and local simulation engine

**M0.1 Product specification.** Specify the first-release journeys and support matrix below before declaring the browser MVP complete.

#### User journeys and acceptance targets

| Journey | Example circuit / action | Completion criteria |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Build and run a first circuit | Open the RC step-response starter, change R or C, run transient analysis, and inspect `V(vout)`. | A first-time user can reach a non-empty result in under 5 minutes with no netlist editing; invalid settings point to the relevant field; the plot reports units and time range. |
| Probe and inspect | Click a wire for node voltage and a two-terminal component for branch current; add both traces to a plot. | Probe names and sign convention are explicit; two traces can be shown together; cursor readout reports value and time; CSV export preserves units/header. |
| Edit and rerun | During or after a run, move/rotate a part or change its value, then rerun. | Edits update connectivity; old results are visibly marked stale and never shown as current; cancel/stop leaves the UI responsive; rerun corresponds to the edited project revision. |
| Import an IC model | Import a user-owned, unencrypted `.SUBCKT` plus a compatible symbol or map its ordered pins in the import dialog. | Pin order follows declared SPICE order; source/provenance and license status are stored; a runnable example reports actionable errors for missing models or unsupported syntax; no vendor model is silently bundled. |
| Save and reopen | Save the example with a custom model and two plot traces, close/reload, then open on a fresh browser profile. | Project, assets, plot setup, and connectivity survive a round trip; the project opens without account/network access; missing external resources are identified by name and hash. |
| Navigate hierarchy | Create a reusable two-level block with named ports, instantiate it twice, and cross-probe into one instance. | Instance parameters and scoped nets remain distinct; the hierarchical circuit simulates equivalently to its flattened reference; navigation returns to the parent without losing selection or view. |

The first browser MVP supports transient analysis for DC sources, resistors, capacitors, inductors, ground, pulse sources, diodes/LEDs, and ideal switches; wire and node-voltage probes; generated netlist preview; local project save/open; and CSV waveform export. AC and DC operating point, parameter sweeps, arbitrary behavioral sources, transistor/op-amp libraries, full model-library management, import/export, hierarchy, cloud collaboration, and native desktop packaging are explicitly outside the first MVP, but remain planned milestones. A component only counts as supported when its ordered electrical pins, editable properties/units, netlist behavior, original symbol, and numeric reference circuit are present.

Acceptance: all six journeys have runnable example projects and automated measurable completion criteria; each MVP matrix item has an owner and is represented in the compatibility matrix.

Hierarchy journey verification (2026-10-13): The Firefox browser journey now runs the two-instance nested divider, selects X2, opens its parameterized child and nested core, probes RCORE, and verifies the saved probe reference is scoped through `example-x2/divider-core-instance`. Returning to Main circuit restores X2’s selection and the saved root viewport. A separate Firefox regression confirms the hierarchical outputs remain equivalent to the flattened circuit. The hierarchy walkthrough banner now lets pointer input pass through its text area to the schematic while keeping its dismiss control interactive.

**M0.2 Source provenance and local-only engine policy.** The project owner selected MIT for ELSim-authored source on 2026-10-10; the repository keeps its source license and third-party notices. ngspice and its toolchain are separate upstream projects. Per the project owner’s current direction, generated ngspice WASM is built locally by each developer, kept ignored under `public/engine/`, and is not committed, built by GitHub Pages, or distributed by this project. Do not make artifact-bound binary redistribution review or license inventory work a blocker for app development or the engine-free Pages preview. Existing notices and npm inventory remain as repository provenance; do not spend feature-work time simplifying that tooling. If the project scope changes to ship a generated engine or third-party models, reopen the relevant distribution review before doing so.

The earlier Pages artifact-bound engine review workflow is superseded by this local-only runtime policy; the current Pages workflow builds only the engine-free editor preview.

**M0.3 Technical spike.** Compile a reproducible local ngspice WASM build with Emscripten; run RC transient, diode DC, RLC AC, and a small `.SUBCKT` example in a worker; obtain numeric vectors and diagnostics; measure load size, load time, memory, cancellation, and UI responsiveness in Chromium and Firefox. Acceptance: results match the same pinned native ngspice within recorded tolerances; failed or stalled runs can be terminated; build recipe works from the documented local toolchain. GitHub Actions no longer builds or tests WASM by owner request. Run `npm run test:wasm` with a native ngspice executable and the engine browser commands locally when changing solver or engine-adapter code. If this fails, record the failure and revise architecture before building the editor.

**M0.4 Architecture contracts.** JSON schemas and the engine protocol define requests, results, run metadata, errors, and netlist source maps. Run records now include solver identity/version, project version, generated netlist/sweep SHA-256, full simulation settings and measurements, and IDs, filenames, and SHA-256 fingerprints for imported model libraries. Model fingerprints are optional at the v1 protocol boundary for older independent producers, but ELSim always emits them. A toolbar button downloads the complete run record as a JSON report with the generated netlist, source map, diagnostics, measurements, trace descriptors, and provenance hashes (waveform samples remain in the app/CSV export). Request/result contract tests reject malformed hashes and preserve compatibility when older producers omit this optional field. A standalone JavaScript peer fixture imports no ELSim protocol code: it produces initialize/run/cancel/dispose requests and ready/progress/result/cancelled/error responses that ELSim validates, while its independently written consumer checks ELSim-shaped responses including finite ordered vectors. Interoperability acceptance is met for the current message set; add peer fixtures whenever the protocol adds message types or fields.

### M1 — Browser MVP: learn, build, simulate

**M1.1 Editor primitives.** Grid, zoom/pan, selection, wire routing/junctions, rotate/flip, move, copy/paste, undo/redo, save/open. Acceptance: keyboard and pointer flows; no accidental electrical connection at crossings; connectivity remains stable through edits and reload.

**M1.1a — Schematic editing and wire-topology overhaul.** Preserve the user-selected simple interaction model: a wire is an independent two-endpoint segment, not a routed path attached to a component. Drawing is one drag between two grid points; connectivity is derived from coincident pins/endpoints, endpoint-on-segment T connections, global net labels, and explicit crossing junctions. Do not add path finding or silently reroute wires. Moving or deleting a component leaves every wire, ground, junction, and label where it is; moving a wire moves only that wire. In a multi-selection drag, move only the objects that were explicitly selected, together. Dragging one wire endpoint changes only that endpoint; make the open end visible and let circuit checks explain the resulting disconnection. Falstad/CircuitJS is a behavioral reference for discoverable controls and feedback only; implement independently and do not copy its source or assets. Keep all ELSim-authored component, wire, label, and junction coordinates on the shared 10-unit grid, including when validating hand-edited project JSON. The current model stores each wire as exactly two endpoints and splits legacy multi-point paths during migration. Connectivity, hit testing, crossing detection, flow sections, and connection dots now operate on each two-endpoint segment directly. Wires can be removed through the common Delete selected action; deleting a component leaves its independent wire geometry open. Unit regressions cover per-operation crossing, junction, endpoint, branch, and legacy-path migration behavior. Nineteen focused Firefox browser regressions now pass for keyboard selection, junction editing, group and mixed selection movement, independent wire geometry, endpoint dragging, connection dots, component rotation, hierarchy pin editing, undo/redo, save/reopen, and wire deletion. Wire movement now keeps selected parts and wire segments together when a mixed selection is dragged. Tests were updated to use two-endpoint SVG lines and the current junction shortcut. The ngspice browser workflows that formerly read the retired bottom trace picker now use the right-click probe chooser and selected waveform windows. Local Firefox verified the probe/window path, current-source DC sweep, imported B-source operating point, and imported op-amp operating point. A Firefox engine run verifies grounded RC return current, equal current split and opposing directions in parallel resistor branches, and KCL-consistent NPN base/collector/emitter currents from ngspice pin vectors. A geometry/value edit regression confirms that moving the source leaves independent wires fixed, hides stale current flow, and that restoring the source plus changing resistance refreshes the current magnitude. Reorientation now uses the same electrical-connectivity comparison as document topology checks: rotating or flipping a connected part is refused when stationary wires/labels would change pin membership, while isolated parts can rotate/flip normally. A Firefox archive round trip moves a wire endpoint, rotates/flips a free part, saves a portable `.elsim`, and reopens it with both the new topology and transforms preserved. Active acceptance passed (2026-10-11): the full non-LTspice engine-backed suite passes all 44 workflows in both Chromium and Firefox. The transient zoom regression waits for the persisted plot range to update after the wheel event, avoiding a timing race. M1.1 schematic editing and the M1.1a wire-topology acceptance are complete for the supported browser matrix; continue broader Firefox engine and large-circuit verification under M4.

Keyboard wire-endpoint editing (2026-10-12): Tab to a wire and press Enter to select it and move focus directly to endpoint 1. Tab reaches endpoint 2; arrow keys move the focused endpoint by one 10-unit grid step; Escape returns focus to the wire. The opposite endpoint stays fixed, repeated keydown events during a held arrow key form one undo step, and pointer dragging remains unchanged. Firefox verifies undo restores the start point and redo restores the moved point. A polite live region announces the endpoint number, movement direction, and new grid position to screen readers. Firefox regressions pass for keyboard selection, pointer endpoint drag, endpoint arrow movement, live announcement, and accessible names.

Hand-edited hierarchy grid coverage (2026-10-12): Opening native ELSim JSON normalizes root and nested-sheet part positions, wire endpoints, labels, and junctions onto the shared 10-unit grid before connectivity validation. A regression perturbs each geometry class in both root and child sheets and checks that the validated project is fully aligned.

Wire-placement hit testing (2026-10-12): Drag endpoint snapping now uses cached spatial indexes for component pins and wire segments rather than rebuilding/scanning every pin and wire on every pointer move. Exact 16-unit pin and 8-unit wire tolerances and existing grid snap behavior are retained. A 2,000-distant-wire domain fixture compares indexed results to the full scan, including long segments; Firefox verifies wire placement and armed tool behavior.

Wire hover and group-copy rendering (2026-10-13): Wire hover z-order is now derived through React's keyed render order instead of moving SVG nodes directly in the DOM. This prevents React from associating selected state with the wrong wire after hover and schematic updates. Firefox verifies that hovering before copying/pasting a full circuit leaves exactly the newly pasted wires selected.

**M1.2 Starter library.** Ground; R, C, L; DC, AC, pulse sources; diode, Zener diode, and LED; switch; VCVS and VCCS; educational NPN and PNP transistors; educational Level 1 NMOS and PMOS devices; educational N-channel and P-channel JFETs; probe; op-amp models where the model is documented and redistributable. Each part has properties, units, validation, symbol, help, and a netlist test. Component cards now expose concise behavior guidance to assistive technology and show it on hover or keyboard focus; the lamp and LED descriptions distinguish their visual animation from thermal/device physics. Built-in transistor cards use original approximate teaching models; neither is a substitute for a device datasheet model or uses vendor model files. A PNP high-side switch example now pairs the built-in PNP model with a pulsed active-low base and a 5 V load supply; Chromium and Firefox verify its solved on/off voltages and current animation. A P-JFET positive-rail example now demonstrates channel conduction at zero gate bias and cutoff from positive gate drive; Chromium and Firefox verify both solved states and the current animation. Polarized capacitors use the same electrical capacitor model with persisted presentation metadata: lowercase `c` places a standard capacitor, uppercase `C` places a polarized one, pin 1/2 markings follow orientation, and the inspector can toggle them. Domain coverage confirms polarity metadata does not change the generated netlist; a focused browser workflow covers placement and editing. The basic library also includes a four-terminal coupled-inductor transformer (`T` shortcut) with editable primary inductance and secondary-to-primary turns ratio. Its original two-winding symbol marks corresponding positive terminals; its two winding glows follow their separately solved currents, and right-click probes expose separate primary- and secondary-winding current traces. Transient result control saves both root winding currents alongside other device currents; hierarchy-scoped saves retain nested winding vectors. AC and noise result control removes current-only save directives so node-voltage vectors remain available for probes. Generated SPICE uses two inductors plus a coupling card. The AC step-up lesson and focused ngspice browser test exercise its solve and primary voltage probe. The primary and secondary windings accept independent series copper resistance; the coupling coefficient models leakage, while core saturation is not modeled; LTspice export reports this component as unsupported while that work is paused. Acceptance: a beginner can build and run LED, RC, RLC, controlled-source, and transistor switch examples without writing SPICE.

The four-pin voltage-controlled switch (`S` shortcut) has editable threshold, on/off resistance, and hysteresis; each instance produces an ngspice `SW` model and has an `I(Sx)` probe. Its contact animation replays the saved control-voltage trace with the same hysteresis thresholds and an explicit OFF initial condition. State histories are computed lazily for visible switches. The pulse-driven example and focused browser test verify output voltage, current probing, and contact state as the time cursor moves, using the existing local WASM build.

The two-pin current-controlled switch (`W` shortcut) is also implemented. It selects an independent voltage source whose branch current controls the contact, uses either an imported CSW model or editable IT/IH/Ron/Roff parameters, exposes its own current probe, and animates open/closed state from the actual saved control-source current while replaying results. The component has no LTspice import/export support while that work is paused.

Current-controlled switch lesson (2026-10-14): Added a guided example with a pulsed drive, series resistor, dedicated 0 V sensing source, and separate 5 V switched load. The 0 V source makes positive conventional branch current explicit; W1 closes above 2 mA and reopens below the 1.9 mA hysteresis boundary. The example plots drive/sense/output voltages plus sensor and switch currents, appears in the Switching category, and explains the separate sensing and load circuits. Domain coverage checks its generated CSW deck and selected traces; Firefox UI coverage checks discovery and guidance, and the existing local ngspice runtime verifies the plotted current, output voltage, cursor-driven contact state, and current magnitude/direction on all eight sensing/load wires in both pulse phases. TypeScript, all 34 unit-test groups, production build/download budget, and the focused Firefox tests pass. No WASM rebuild or hosted workflow ran.

Logic-gate components now include an inverter and configurable two-input AND, OR, NAND, NOR, XOR, and XNOR gates. Their ordered input/output pins are part of the component schema, and they netlist as ideal behavioral voltage sources. New gates default to 2.5 V input thresholds and 0/5 V outputs; each gate and inverter lets users set its own threshold and low/high output levels, including 3.3 V logic. No XSPICE build option is needed. Gate symbols distinguish AND, OR, and XOR bodies, including the XOR extra input curve and inversion bubbles. The output lead indicates solved low/high state and follows the shared simulation cursor; its accessible label reports the sampled state. Right-clicking a gate or inverter offers a direct output-to-ground voltage probe alongside pin-to-pin choices. The guided “Logic gate basics” lesson drives every gate with two pulse sources and exposes all four truth-table combinations. Domain tests check gate netlists and reject invalid threshold/level ordering; focused Chromium and Firefox browser workflows verify symbol families, truth tables, custom 3.3 V levels, output indicators, and a gate-output waveform through the existing local ngspice runtime. LTspice export reports these ideal models as unsupported rather than silently changing their behavior.

**M1.3 Simulation controls.** Run, result-playback pause, reset, transient and DC operating point, simulation settings, error-to-object mapping, and nonconvergence guidance. Pause stops only playback of solved samples; it does not pause or suspend ngspice, and the toolbar labels this explicitly. **Status: implemented for the current browser MVP.** A global Celsius temperature setting is implemented and saved with projects; non-default values emit one top-level `.temp` directive, and validation rejects values below absolute zero or above 10000 °C. The 27 °C default keeps starter netlists unchanged. A focused Firefox/ngspice browser workflow verifies that the directive runs successfully. Acceptance: edits invalidate stale results; diagnostics point to a component or directive when possible. Preflight validation selects components and focuses invalid fields before model-fingerprint or engine-availability checks. Malformed numeric component values and voltage/current pulse levels or timing values map to their inspector fields; inline errors have accessible descriptions, and focus runs after the selected inspector renders. Chromium verifies invalid pulse timing and correction. Circuit checks also flag malformed independent DC voltage-source values while accepting a declared sheet parameter, and identify DC sweeps that target a missing or non-source part; domain tests cover signed values, parameter resolution, unit mismatch, and both valid and invalid voltage/current sweep targets. Result status toasts no longer intercept pointer input over the workspace while their dismiss button remains interactive; the RC browser workflow verifies trace selection and CSV export after a result toast appears. Voltage/current PWL point count, time, and amplitude validation now maps to the source inspector; Chromium verifies malformed voltage PWL time selection/focus and correction. The browser worker now captures actionable ngspice errors from stderr as well as stdout, maps the “line no.” format to the netlist source map, and gives an imported model a specific hint when it calls an unavailable behavioral function. A Chromium test exercises the pinned WASM engine and verifies the original model filename and line. Fatal timestep/convergence messages without an `Error:` prefix are now detected and surfaced with recovery hints; recoverable source-stepping warnings are not misreported as fatal. Domain tests cover both cases, and the imported-model worker diagnostic passes in Chromium and Firefox. Imported model diagnostics now give distinct guidance for unknown parameters, device/model type mismatches, malformed parameter lists or card syntax, missing referenced models/subcircuits (including “model X not found” and “could not/couldn’t find model X” wording), and convergence failures attributed to an imported model line while retaining the original message and source location; domain tests cover these cases. When ngspice emits `strtod: Invalid argument` without a source line, diagnostics identify the sole imported model library when unambiguous, state that the exact card/line was not identified, and offer the existing replace-library action; with multiple imported libraries they avoid guessing. Unit tests cover both cases, and Firefox exercises the actual WASM parser failure. Successful runs expose solver version, run ID, run settings, netlist/sweep fingerprint, and imported model hashes through a downloadable JSON run report that makes those inputs and diagnostics portable without embedding potentially large waveform vectors.

The worker’s “no writable vector” failure now gives the same useful recovery hint as other unavailable output vectors: check whether the selected analysis creates that voltage/current vector and whether the referenced node or component exists. A focused diagnostics unit test covers this case. After AC/noise results, right-clicking a component explains that current traces were not saved and suggests transient or DC analysis instead of offering a probe that will fail; the Firefox transformer workflow verifies this while preserving voltage probes.

Fatal solver diagnostic follow-up (2026-10-12): The ngspice worker now recognizes both `Fatal:` and `Fatal error:` prefixes, keeps the fatal text instead of reducing it to an exit-code message, and maps an attached `line no.` location through the netlist source map. A focused domain regression covers stderr output and source selection; all 28 unit groups and TypeScript checking pass. No engine rebuild is needed.

Transient data-window control (2026-10-12): Transient settings can optionally set ngspice `tstart` to discard stored waveform samples before a chosen time. The circuit still solves from zero so startup behavior remains electrically correct; the setting is validated against `stopTime`, saved in native project files, and exposed as “Start saving at” with an explanation in the inspector. A focused Firefox UI regression checks persistence and generated netlist output; no WASM rebuild is needed.

Transient resolution presets keep their maximum step based on the full `0`-to-`tstop` solve even when `tstart` discards earlier output. This avoids increasing solver work during startup just to target a denser retained interval; domain and focused Firefox UI regressions protect the full-duration resolution behavior.

Imported-model recovery follow-up (2026-10-12): An ngspice model-card error with a mapped source line now has a Review source line action that opens the original imported library at the failing line and focuses it. A focused Firefox run uses the existing local runtime and checks the source highlight and focus.

Mapped solver-error navigation (2026-10-12): When an ngspice diagnostic maps to a top-level component, the editor now selects it and pans it into the center of the schematic view. A focused Firefox UI regression injects a worker error for an offscreen component and verifies both selection and viewport centering.

Nested-sheet diagnostic navigation (2026-10-12): Preflight errors from components inside nested reusable sheets now resolve their `sheetId:partId` source path against the visible sheet: when viewing an ancestor, the editor selects the next reusable-block instance toward the error; when viewing the source sheet, it selects the failing component itself. Both targets are centered in the schematic. Two focused Firefox regressions use the real nested-divider example with an invalid resistor value and verify selection/centering in both editor contexts. Typecheck passes; no WASM or production build was needed.

Imported-model diagnostic follow-up (2026-10-12): Checked actual failures against the already-built local ngspice 47 runtime. The worker now preserves specific preceding warnings such as “can’t find model” and “model type mismatch” when ngspice follows them with a generic “Error on line …” message, maps that line to the schematic device, and explains wrong device/model types and unresolved model parameters. Focused `strtod: Invalid argument` guidance now covers both imported-card source locations and fatal output without a line number; regressions preserve the observed fatal text and verify both hint paths. Regression inputs mirror observed stderr text. Typecheck and all 28 unit groups pass; no WASM rebuild was needed.

**UI baseline — waveform and simulation controls (preserve in future work).** The main workspace toolbar owns transient result playback: Play/Pause and Rewind control result animation (they do not pause or rerun ngspice), and the simulation-time scrubber sits beside those controls in the main window. The waveform window is for plots only and stays visually simple; playback controls and the “results are stale” warning belong in the main workspace toolbar. The desktop stale-results message overlays below the toolbar without changing its height or moving controls; mobile uses its dedicated notice strip. Waveforms open from a right-click probe action on a wire or component; users can add a selected voltage/current trace to the current plot window or open a new one. Only explicitly selected traces appear in plots; there is no “Choose traces” list and no automatically populated all-components voltage/current panel. Each selected trace can be disabled/enabled or removed. Keep stable reserved space for probe controls/labels so toggling traces does not shift the plot; do not show the redundant “Positive component…” label. Plot content must resize and redraw with its window and viewport; axes, labels, grid, and waveforms must stay scaled together. Horizontal zoom is controlled by the scroll wheel around the pointer; preserve readable and correctly scaled axis values when opening or resizing a plot. Context menus and toolbar settings popovers dismiss when clicking outside them. This is the accepted layout and interaction contract, not an invitation to redesign the waveform UI. If a future request conflicts with this baseline, ask before changing it.

Floating waveform windows must open below the main workspace toolbar and tiling must keep them there, so plots never cover simulation playback controls. Keep browser feedback loops short: `npm run test:e2e:engine` / `:engine:firefox` run the focused eight-case `@engine-smoke` set (first-circuit solve and rerun, voltage probes, branch-current animation, waveform windows, imported-IC save/reopen, and AC/DC plot interaction); use `npm run test:e2e:engine:full` / `:full:firefox` for the complete engine-backed suite at solver or milestone checkpoints. The eight-case Firefox smoke set passed in 25.7 seconds against the already-built local WASM runtime. The full Firefox engine collection currently contains 94 tests; the smoke tag only selects representative fast feedback cases, and every other engine workflow remains tagged `@ngspice` for the full run. Neither command compiles WASM. This iteration shortcut does not replace the full-suite acceptance gate for M1.1a.

**M1.4 Results and visualization.** Click-to-probe voltage/current, synchronized time cursor, dockable plot panels, plot axes/units, multiple traces, zoom, cursors, and CSV export. Implement current flow, LED/lamp, capacitor, and inductor visual channels based on results. The workspace supports one detached waveform panel plus up to eight additional movable, resizable plot panels; each extra panel stores its own trace selection and horizontal range while sharing the solved animation cursor. AC and DC sweep plots also share a result-scoped cursor coordinate across windows, mapping the same frequency or swept value to each window's nearest sample; Firefox verifies synchronized cursor readouts across separate AC windows. Chromium verifies panel creation, independent trace selection, and saved layout restoration. A floating plot can dock into the primary Waveforms pane, transferring its trace selection and current-analysis zoom range into primary project settings; the RC browser workflow checks the transfer, persisted settings, and rendered selection. The new Tile open plot windows action lays out all visible floating plots in a saved grid, keeps the main detached waveform window in sync with auxiliary panels, and leaves each window independently movable/resizable; a Chromium regression checks geometry persistence and rendered positions. Dragging a plot title to an edge of another plot now splits that window's space and saves both bounds; the dropped plot retains its independent trace and range settings, and narrow targets adapt the split direction when the requested axis cannot fit. The RC browser workflow exercises pointer-driven docking into the detached main waveform panel and verifies persisted adjacent pane geometry. Cursor movement reuses memoized waveform polyline geometry, so million-sample vectors are not rescanned or reformatted when only the animation cursor changes. The parser retains an offset-based ASCII fallback for compatibility, while the production worker requests binary ngspice raw output and parses typed numeric payloads directly; the 1,034,006-sample benchmark reduced result size from 55.8 MB to 16.5 MB and parse time from 476 ms to 17 ms. Local Playwright regressions pass when the workspace grants loopback binding; the restricted sandbox can reject Vite startup without that permission. A shared cursor external store updates only the playback toolbar, schematic animation, and open plots rather than rerendering the full editor. Downsampled extrema indices and visible value ranges are shared across plot windows when they display the same immutable result-vector range; both per-vector caches are bounded to 16 ranges and have unit regressions for reuse and range isolation. The focused multi-window benchmark uses four plot windows with four traces each (16 rendered traces) and solves 1,000,130 transient samples. A post-parser benchmark recorded Firefox at 19.048 s and Chromium at 6.973 s, with repeated scrub updates of 39.93–76.27 ms and 28.60–35.63 ms respectively; these are single local observations, not stable timing claims. These are measurements, not CI limits. A development-only `VITE_ELSIM_PROFILE=1` worker trace attributes about 90% of the 17.9 s Firefox worker run to ngspice `callMain` in the current `-O2` build. Plot wheel zoom and drag-pan now update each graph locally and persist the final horizontal range after 120 ms of inactivity, avoiding project-wide state updates on every gesture frame; toolbar zoom/reset remain immediate. A focused Firefox/ngspice regression confirms the range remains saved across pan, zoom, and simulation reruns, and all 119 Firefox UI-smoke cases pass. A five-run Firefox cursor-update repeat on the 501-component / 1,001-vector fixture measured 41.36–61.49 ms for the Playwright scrub interval in the normal viewport and 87.20–94.58 ms with all components fitted; those intervals include automation/assertion work. Instrumented updates reached the next animation frame in 0–1 ms and the second frame in 3–20 ms across both layouts. This did not show a missed first frame or justify a renderer change; details are in `PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`. Remaining: compare an `-O3` runtime build and reduce Firefox's solver time only if profiling supports it. Emscripten is not installed on the current development host (verified 2026-10-24); use the existing `-O2` runtime for UI and solver validation, and run the comparison only when the toolchain is available. WASM linear-memory capacity is measured directly in the browser worker for Chromium and Firefox; total browser/worker allocation remains open under M4.3. Acceptance: plots and animations derive from the same run and cursor; high-sample-count traces remain responsive.

M1.4 floating-window drag regression (2026-10-24): A Firefox/ngspice smoke test drags a waveform title across workspace text, confirms the window moves by the pointer delta, and verifies the drag leaves no text selection. It passes with the existing title-bar selection rule; no redundant drag-state code was added.

Waveform persistence after an electrical edit (2026-10-24): The Firefox/ngspice rerun workflow now changes R1 after zooming, panning, moving both measurement cursors, and selecting a component-voltage trace. After the changed circuit solves, it verifies the plot range, value ranges, cursor position, selected trace IDs, and each trace’s assigned color remain unchanged. The focused run passes with the existing local runtime; no WASM rebuild or Pages workflow ran.

Paired waveform measurement cursors (2026-10-24): Transient, AC, noise, and DC-sweep plots keep Cursor A synchronized with playback or the shared analysis cursor and can add a plot-local Cursor B. Selecting either cursor makes chart clicks and arrow-key navigation move that cursor; readouts show both sampled values plus signed Δt, Δf, sweep-axis delta, and per-trace ΔV/ΔI or AC/noise deltas with units. Phase differences wrap to the signed ±180° interval. The controls row is always reserved and remains above the plot during resize. Four focused Firefox/ngspice workflows cover transient, AC magnitude/phase, noise density, and mixed voltage/current DC-sweep cursors using the existing local runtime.

Cursor scrub baseline (2026-10-14): Three fresh-context runs of the 500-component animation benchmark measured 34.06 ms median in Chromium (33.32–37.07 ms) and 49.89 ms in Firefox (47.57–50.59 ms). The earlier same-host Firefox median was 52.54 ms across two runs; the overlap does not establish an improvement. This measurement includes Playwright keyboard and assertion time, so a controlled rendering-only before/after comparison remains open. The benchmark reused the existing local WASM runtime.

Current component slice: a Lamp card uses the resistor electrical model and a configurable nominal animation power. Its warm bulb glow scales from solved absolute voltage × branch current and is capped for readability; the inspector states that no filament or thermal behavior is simulated. Lamp values participate in ordinary resistance checks, sweeps, and netlisting. LTspice export writes the resistance with an R symbol and reports the omitted ELSim animation metadata. Numeric tests verify resistor netlisting and nominal-power validation; export tests verify the resistor mapping and loss report.

Capacitor result feedback now marks the positive and negative plates from the solved voltage polarity and scales their glow with voltage magnitude. Its accessible SVG title reports `Q = C × V` in coulombs when capacitance is a numeric scalar; the RC browser workflow verifies the polarity marks and charge readout at the selected final sample. This is a cursor-linked electrical quantity, not a separate simulated charge trajectory.

Inductor result feedback uses a coil glow whose strength follows solved current; the accessible SVG title reports magnetic field energy `E = ½ L I²` for numeric inductance. Pin-to-pin current remains available as a waveform probe. The RLC ngspice browser workflow checks the energy readout at the scrubbed result sample.

NPN/PNP collector and NMOS/PMOS drain branches show a restrained solver-driven channel-current indicator; current magnitude and direction are represented by the wire-flow dots, which stay on wires. Accessible component descriptions and current probes continue to report solved branch values. NPN, NMOS, and PMOS ngspice examples verify the result-linked indicators at scrubbed active-state samples; these visuals do not change device behavior.

The RC first-use browser workflow also exports the selected `V(vin)` and `V(vout)` traces and reads the downloaded CSV to check time and voltage units in the header plus populated sample rows. This exercises the visible export action in addition to the serializer’s domain tests.

Transient, operating-point, AC, and DC-sweep trace selectors expose named groups and `aria-pressed` state, so screen readers can distinguish plotted traces from available traces. When a result has more than 12 vectors, a labeled search filters the visible trace controls by vector name while preserving plot selection; for results above 100 vectors, the list renders the first 100 and progressively adds more on request to avoid creating thousands of buttons at once. The Chromium and Firefox 500-instance hierarchy workflow searches for `I(r.x500.rmain)`, checks the no-match state, and verifies the 100-item initial page plus progressive expansion against the 1,003-result circuit.

Result ingestion now verifies that each waveform axis is finite and strictly increasing. If an independent engine returns traces on different adaptive grids, ELSim resamples them onto the primary trace's axis over their common time/frequency/sweep range, including real and imaginary AC values. The cursor, schematic animation, and trace readouts therefore refer to the same solved independent-variable value; disjoint ranges fail with an explicit diagnostic rather than silently clamping. Engine-protocol tests cover interpolation, common-range clipping, complex-value alignment, disjoint ranges, and unordered-axis rejection.

Voltage-source instances now choose Pulse, DC, or bounded PWL behavior for transient analysis. PWL time/voltage fields validate numeric input, nonnegative time, and strict ordering inline with accessible field errors; browser coverage verifies the error clears after a corrected point. Native voltage/current PWL tables support 2–256 points and add/remove controls; finite repeat expansion remains limited to 32 ngspice points. Pulse sources edit initial level, delay, rise/fall time, on-time, and period. Existing projects without these fields retain the standard pulse behavior. AC magnitude and phase can be set per voltage or current source, with the voltage project magnitude as a fallback; LTspice `Value2 AC` is imported and exported with per-source settings. LTspice scalar sources import as DC and seven-argument PULSE sources preserve their levels and timing. Shared pre-run validation catches invalid AC magnitude/phase, selects the offending source, and focuses the corresponding inspector field; domain coverage verifies invalid and corrected values, and Chromium browser regression verifies invalid AC phase selection/focus plus a corrected AC run. Netlisting also validates these values. The NPN switch example uses a pulsed base drive with a steady 5 V supply, and browser coverage verifies both the on and off collector states.

Maximum plot-window profile (2026-10-12): Firefox opened the supported maximum of eight waveform windows with four traces each against one 1,000,130-sample transient. The run completed in 19.07 s; End/Home/End scrubs measured 70.52 / 52.23 / 46.91 ms, versus 20.06 s and 45.49 / 27.78 / 28.81 ms for four windows and 16 traces on the same host. Result-ready times were similar in these single runs; scrub latency increased with rendered traces while the largest main-thread timer gap remained 48 ms. No local WASM rebuild was needed. See `PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md` for limits of this comparison.

Wire playback hot-path cleanup (2026-10-12): the list of animated wires is now memoized across simulation-cursor updates, avoiding a full-schematic wire scan on every playback sample. All 28 unit groups and a focused Firefox/ngspice animation regression pass; no WASM rebuild was needed.
Eight-window Firefox cursor refresh (2026-10-12): Two focused repeats solved 1,000,130 samples and rendered 32 traces in eight plot windows. Result-ready time was 9.99/10.08 s; the final warmed cursor refresh was 31/32 ms, while the first two refreshes were 85–101 ms. Firefox exposed no heap metric. This verifies repeated refreshes but is not a clea before/after comparison against the earlier 16-trace profile; the rendering-gain question remains open. The existing local WASM was reused.
Shared-axis cursor readout fast path (2026-10-12): Worker-produced traces share one immutable axis array, so cursor readouts now use the selected sample index directly and retain binary search only for distinct external axes. Typecheck and the focused Firefox million-sample/eight-window benchmark pass. The warmed update measured 31.02 ms versus 31.03/32.04 ms immediately before; this is within noise and does not establish a visible gain. Broader rendering comparisons remain open.
Cursor update isolation (2026-10-12): The transient plot shell no longer subscribes directly to the simulation cursor. The marker, time label, and readout update independently while static plot controls and geometry avoid parent-component rerenders. One follow-up eight-window Firefox run measured similar result-ready time and lower second/third scrub latency, but single-run variance is too high to claim a stable gain. Repeat profiling remains open; details are in `PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`.


Capacitor charge animation regression (2026-10-12): Firefox now runs the existing local ngspice runtime, scrubs a solved transient from 10 ms to 30 ms, and verifies both the visible capacitor plate extension and glow scale increase with the solved charge Q = C × V. The focused test passes in 4.5 seconds without rebuilding WASM.

Voltage-controlled switch current-flow follow-up (2026-10-12): The wire sampler now maps `I(Sx)` onto conduction pins 1–2 and leaves control pins 3–4 at zero current. The switch example now draws explicit feed, output, and ground-return wire segments, so the full solved load current is visible along its path. Unit coverage and the focused Firefox/ngspice switch workflow verify all five wire segments animate at the expected current.

Controlled-source current-flow follow-up (2026-10-12): CCCS/CCVS branch currents now drive their two output-pin wire paths; ideal inverter and logic-gate current is assigned to the output pin while input pins remain at zero. The current-controlled-source lesson now draws explicit sensor, load, and return wires, with output paths routed around the opposite terminals to preserve topology. Unit tests cover output-versus-input flow, and the Firefox/ngspice lesson verifies both controlled-source loops animate after solving.

Wire animation idle-work fix (2026-10-12): The shared `requestAnimationFrame` loop now runs only while at least one registered visible wire carries nonzero solved current; it stops as soon as all active currents fall below the visibility threshold and resumes when current returns. A unit regression verifies idle startup, prompt stopping, and resumption. This prevents large idle circuits from consuming animation-frame work without changing result sampling or dot speed/direction.

High-sample profile refresh (2026-10-14): The worker now forwards the development-only performance profile over the existing progress channel so browser automation can capture it consistently. The existing `-O2` runtime solved 1,000,130 samples and rendered eight plot windows/32 traces in 4.02 s in Chromium and 10.04 s in Firefox; cursor scrubs were 29–42 ms and 36–60 ms respectively, with only 8–9 ms of timer excess during cursor movement. Profile details and memory limits are recorded in `PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`. No WASM rebuild was run. The requested `-O3` comparison remains open because Emscripten is not installed in this environment.

Transient plot range-scan fusion (2026-10-14): Each visible vector’s exact auto-range is now computed from the same extrema-preserving indices used to render it, avoiding a second full-vector scan. Unit coverage confirms a million-sample trace’s isolated positive/negative extrema remain exact. Three same-host Chromium runs show the solver-dominated total time is unchanged within run variance (3.95–4.01 s); the optimization reduces redundant sample reads but is not claimed as a measured wall-clock gain. No WASM rebuild was run.

Waveform pan reliability (2026-10-14): Plot drags now track pointer movement at the window level so raising a floating plot cannot cancel its drag when React reorders the window. The focused regression checks that horizontal drag changes the visible time range while vertical drag leaves the shared value axes fixed. Browser-test setup now waits for autosave completion before seeding project data, avoiding page-hide writes racing test fixtures. Unit, typecheck, production build, and the 132-case non-engine browser suite were run; 128 passed before four isolated test setup/assertion fixes, and those four now pass in isolation. No WASM rebuild was needed.

**M1.5 Beginner experience.** Onboarding, searchable components, example circuits, hover explanations, sensible simulation presets, and accessible colors/keyboard operations. Acceptance: scripted first-use test reaches a working circuit without knowledge of netlists.

**Status: complete for the current first-use MVP.** The seven-example “Start here” path, searchable/filterable component and example libraries, component behavior help, interactive prediction lessons, Quick tour, simulation presets, keyboard navigation, visible focus, accessible control names, and responsive first-use layout are implemented. The Firefox first-circuit acceptance workflow edits the RC starter, runs ngspice, and inspects `V(vout)` without opening a netlist; it passes with the existing local runtime. The 95-case engine-independent Firefox UI-smoke suite also passes. Manual assistive-technology review remains tracked separately under M4.1.

Component shortcut discovery (2026-10-24): The component library search accepts `key:<shortcut>` (for example, `key:s`) and preserves case so distinct Falstad-style uppercase/lowercase placement keys find the intended part. The Firefox palette regression checks `key:s` and `key:S` resolve to different switch components. Existing category tests no longer rely on fixed card totals as the component catalog grows. No solver/WASM rebuild or Pages workflow ran.

LC energy exchange lesson (2026-10-12): Added a charged series RLC-free LC transient with a small 1 Ω damping resistor; its initial capacitor voltage is 5 V and it plots V(VC) with I(L1), showing the capacitor charge and inductor current trade off over an approximately 6.3 ms period. The focused Firefox/ngspice workflow checks the solved run and charge animation at a quarter-cycle. This fills the reactive-circuit learning gap between standalone capacitor discharge, inductor ramp, and damped RLC ringing.

Example discovery (2026-10-12; updated 2026-10-14): the example picker has a seven-circuit “Start here” path ordered from an interactive switch-and-lamp circuit through Ohm’s law, RC charging, a voltage divider, series-resistor voltage drops, LED current limiting, and capacitor discharge. This puts a direct voltage/current/resistance experiment before the time-dependent lessons and introduces Kirchhoff’s voltage law before diode applications. Topic filters follow their declared order rather than catalog order. A Firefox regression checks the path order and confirms the full catalog remains reachable.

Ohm’s-law prediction activity (2026-10-14): Before running the beginner operating-point example, learners can enter a current estimate in milliamps and check it against the current supply/resistor values. Feedback reveals the I = V/R calculation; changing R1 resets the answer and derives the new expected current. The simulation action remains available without answering the prompt. A Firefox UI regression checks incorrect and correct estimates, recalculation after changing R1, and the 390 px workspace without horizontal page scrolling.

Voltage-divider prediction activity (2026-10-14): The next “Start here” lesson now asks learners to estimate VOUT before the operating-point run, then reveals the divider-ratio calculation. Editing either resistor resets the answer and recalculates the expected midpoint. Both lessons share one accessible numeric prediction control, which can be checked by button or Enter; focused Firefox regressions cover wrong/right estimates and edited resistor values, while the existing Firefox/ngspice lesson workflow confirms the output still solves to 5 V and 6.667 V after the guided edit.

RC time-constant prediction activity (2026-10-14): The third “Start here” lesson asks learners to predict τ = R1 × C1 before running, then relates one time constant to the capacitor reaching about 63% of its final voltage. Editing R1 or C1 resets the estimate and recalculates τ. A focused Firefox UI regression checks wrong/right predictions, recalculation after a resistor edit, the still-available Run action, and the 390 px layout; the existing Firefox/ngspice RC lesson continues to verify the time constant and solved waveform.

Capacitor-discharge prediction activity (2026-10-14): Before running the sixth “Start here” lesson, learners estimate VOUT after 10 ms from the initial capacitor voltage and τ = R1 × C1. The lesson reveals the exponential discharge calculation after checking, and edits to the initial voltage, R1, or C1 reset the estimate. A focused Firefox UI regression checks incorrect/correct answers and recalculation after changing R1; the existing Firefox/ngspice test checks the actual solved decay before and after that resistor edit.

LED current-limiter prediction activity (2026-10-14): Before running the fifth “Start here” lesson, learners estimate branch current from the supply, series resistance, and an explicitly approximate 2 V LED drop. Feedback shows the Ohm’s-law calculation and warns that the real ngspice LED model can differ; changing the source or resistor resets the estimate. A focused Firefox UI regression checks wrong/right guesses, the approximation note, recalculation after changing R1, and the still-available Run action. The existing ngspice LED lesson continues to compare its solved branch current with the resistor-based estimate.

Open-switch prediction activity (2026-10-14): The first “Start here” lesson now asks whether current flows while S1 is open, before running the circuit. Feedback explains the broken loop and guides the user to close the switch and compare the lamp’s solver-driven response. A focused Firefox regression checks both answer paths and confirms Run remains available; the existing Firefox/ngspice workflow continues to verify the switch opens and closes the solved lamp-current path.

Beginner-first example catalog (2026-10-14): The seven “Start here” circuits now appear first even when the full catalog is selected; the dialog explains both ways to browse. All examples remain searchable and available under “All.” Firefox verifies the ordered seven, topic filtering, combined search, and full catalog. This keeps novice guidance visible without hiding advanced circuits.

Example confirmation follow-up (2026-10-12): the replace-circuit prompt now reads the selected example's title from the same catalog used by the picker, removing the duplicated per-example title conditional and keeping the confirmation text aligned as examples are added or renamed. TypeScript checking passes.

Top-bar cleanup (2026-10-12): removed the inactive Share placeholder and decorative “B” avatar button, which looked interactive but had no behavior. The mobile toolbar no longer hides the Quick tour control based on the old Share button's position. Typecheck and the focused Firefox 390 px workspace/simulation workflow pass.

The logic-gate examples now include a 3.3 V variant: 0/3.3 V pulse inputs, a 1.65 V input threshold, and 0/3.3 V outputs. It exercises the user-editable logic levels with a solver-backed beginner example; domain netlist and focused Chromium/Firefox browser coverage verify this configuration.

The voltage/current probe destination dialog now takes keyboard focus when it opens, traps Tab and Shift+Tab within its controls, and returns focus to the probed schematic object when dismissed or after opening a plot. The focused Chromium waveform-window workflow verifies initial focus, both tab-wrap boundaries, and focus restoration using the existing local ngspice runtime.

The example library now includes a static 5 V LED current-limiter lesson alongside the pulse-driven LED example. It starts with a 330 Ω series resistor, selects LED current and output voltage, and guides the learner to increase resistance and compare the current. Domain netlist coverage and a focused browser ngspice smoke test protect the example.

Current slice: the toolbar wraps at browser widths up to 1165 px so Run and analysis controls remain reachable without horizontal page scrolling. At 854 px, the component cards wrap their labels and the RC starter runs in-browser; the waveform plot, solved-time cursor, current flow, and capacitor charge animation update from ngspice vectors. The component library has working All parts, Basics, Semiconductors, and Controlled sources categories; search spans all categories and browser coverage checks category counts and cross-category results. Categories remain visible in the phone-width Components panel. Component cards provide concise, data-backed help on hover and keyboard focus, with accessible names for screen-reader users; Chromium verifies both tooltip activation paths. At phone widths, a workspace tab bar switches between Components, Schematic, and Properties rather than squeezing all three panels. Chromium verified the panel switcher at 390 px, no horizontal document scrolling, and a completed RC simulation with visible transient results. Transient simulation settings now offer Quick, Standard, and Detailed presets targeting about 250, 1,000, and 5,000 samples across the selected stop time; presets update and save the maximum step while leaving direct SPICE entry available. Unit tests validate engineering suffixes and generated steps, and Chromium verifies recalculation after the stop time changes. The five-step quick tour describes the currently open circuit rather than assuming the RC starter, then explains editing, running, reading plots, and saving/reopening `.elsim` projects; it notes that simulation requires the ngspice runtime. Chromium verifies the full tour sequence, focus entry, Tab trapping, focus restoration, and Escape dismissal. Its simulation step now offers a direct “Run this circuit now” action that closes the modal and starts the current project in the local ngspice worker; a Chromium regression verifies the guided action completes a transient solve and exposes `V(vout)`. Example lesson cards now include a direct “Run this circuit” action, show a disabled progress state while solving, and change to “Run this circuit again” when existing results are stale. After a successful, non-stale run, they add an analysis-specific “Try this next” hint for transient scrubbing, AC/DC plot cursors, or operating-point table/right-click probing. Firefox verifies the voltage-divider lesson can start its own simulation and then presents the operating-point follow-up. The first-use Playwright journey changes R1 through the inspector, confirms the project state is saved, then runs the circuit and inspects the solved `V(vout)` waveform without editing a netlist; this focused workflow passed against the local ngspice runtime. Global keyboard shortcuts are ignored while app dialogs are open, and Escape dismisses the quick tour, circuit checks, Examples, Sheet settings, and netlist dialogs. Browser coverage verifies shortcut suppression and Escape dismissal in Examples. Examples, Circuit checks, Sheet settings, the quick tour, model-section import, project and LTspice export, and generated-netlist dialogs now use a shared visibility-aware Tab/Shift+Tab focus loop; dialogs start focus on a close or safe action, including the busy export state. Browser regressions verify loop boundaries in the tour and LTspice export preview, plus focus return for Examples, Circuit checks, Sheet settings, the project rights warning, and LTspice export close/download actions. Schematic parts now expose one roving tab stop, directional arrow-key spatial navigation, accessible names, and Enter/Space activation; Chromium and Firefox browser regressions verify initial focus, movement, and selection. Wire paths and visible net labels are now named keyboard controls with selected state and focus styling; Enter/Space selects them, Delete removes the selection, and Shift+Arrow nudges the selected path or label by one grid step. Chromium and Firefox verify wire/label selection, keyboard nudging, and the existing component navigation flow. Uppercase `C` now places a polarized capacitor while lowercase `c` remains the standard capacitor; its pin polarity markings persist, follow orientation, and can be turned off in the inspector.

Quick-tour announcement (2026-10-12): The changing step heading is now a polite, atomic live region so screen readers announce the new topic while focus remains on the tour controls. The existing Firefox tour flow verifies the live-region attributes alongside step navigation and focus trapping.

The Examples dialog includes an RLC ringing circuit, a voltage-controlled sources comparison (gain-of-two VCVS and 1 mS VCCS), a current-controlled sources comparison (gain-of-two CCCS and 2 kΩ CCVS driven by the same 1 mA sense current), an NPN low-side switch, a PNP high-side switch with active-low base drive, an NMOS low-side switch with a separate gate resistor, 5 V load supply, and bulk tied to ground, a PMOS high-side switch with active-low gate drive and source/bulk tied to the 5 V supply, and an N-JFET low-side switch demonstrating zero-bias conduction and cutoff under negative gate bias, and a P-JFET positive-rail switch demonstrating conduction at zero gate bias and cutoff under positive gate bias. Domain regressions verify generated decks; Chromium browser coverage runs these examples with WASM and checks their output voltages at the waveform cursor. The NMOS Level 1 teaching model produces partial conduction with the example's 5 V gate drive, so the on-state drain is about 4.9 V rather than a rail-to-ground ideal switch; browser coverage documents that behavior and confirms the drain returns to 5 V when the gate is low. The N-JFET browser workflow checks both drain states and confirms the component current animation follows the solved result. The WASM fixture suite now verifies both NJF and PJF operating-point current vectors. Guided examples now cover the R, C, L, diode/LED, E/G/F/H controlled-source, NPN/PNP, NJFET/PJFET, NMOS, PMOS, and hierarchy paths. The RLC ringing lesson now explains that its 5 Ω/10 mH/100 µF series circuit is underdamped and calculates the critical series resistance (about 20 Ω) from the current L and C. After an R1 edit, stale plots are labeled with current values and a guided rerun teaches the near-critical response; the lesson then suggests increasing resistance to compare overdamping. A focused Firefox/ngspice workflow verifies the initial classification, stale guidance, rerun, and updated damping prediction using the local runtime. The P-JFET browser workflow verifies the 5 V positive-rail operating states in Chromium and Firefox. Example loading now opens a dismissible learning card with a device-specific goal and a concrete result to inspect; project open, LTspice import, and New clear that guidance. The PNP high-side switch example is browser-verified in Chromium and Firefox, including active-low base behavior and solved current animation. Example constructors live in a dynamically loaded module. React and icon dependencies are separate hashed production chunks: the main application chunk is 402.32 kB (112.70 kB gzip), React is 186.62 kB (58.52 kB gzip), icons are 17.74 kB (3.74 kB gzip), and the dynamically loaded examples chunk is 19.25 kB (3.69 kB gzip). This removes the oversized-chunk warning and lets vendor chunks cache independently; because the shell preloads them, the split does not reduce total initial compressed JavaScript. The generated service worker precaches all chunks, and the production PWA regression opens the app while offline. Broader beginner walkthroughs and additional library examples remain. A diode half-wave rectifier example demonstrates AC input, diode conduction, and negative-half-cycle blocking; its netlist has domain coverage, and Chromium verifies both half-cycles against ngspice WASM. A Zener regulator lesson adds an original 5.1 V reverse-breakdown teaching model, the `z` placement shortcut, imported D-model selection, a 12 V series-resistor example, and solver-backed output/current probes; domain and Firefox regressions verify the generated model, current vector, and output near 5.1 V. The guided lesson predicts resistor current from the supply, 5.1 V clamp, and R1, then shows current/output values while results are stale and after a direct rerun. The Firefox workflow doubles R1 and verifies regulation remains near 5.1 V, then raises it to 10 kΩ and confirms ngspice drops below the model’s 1 mA knee and loses regulation. A guided RC low-pass AC example sweeps 10 Hz–100 kHz and teaches the 1 kHz, approximately −3 dB cutoff; netlist and numeric WASM fixtures verify the 0.707 V/V response, and a focused Chromium workflow runs the example and plots both traces. After solving, both low-pass and high-pass lessons calculate the cutoff from the edited R and C values and suggest doubling C to halve that frequency. The Firefox low-pass workflow edits C from 159 nF to 318 nF, reruns ngspice, and uses the keyboard cursor to verify approximately −3 dB near 501 Hz; the high-pass browser workflow checks the initial prediction. Its complementary guided RC high-pass example uses the same 1 kHz corner, with a netlist test, a numeric WASM fixture that checks low-frequency rejection, the 0.707 V/V corner, and high-frequency pass-through, plus a Chromium workflow that runs and plots both traces. The Examples dialog now filters its guided circuits by title and explanation, with keyboard-accessible search, clear control, and a no-results message; a focused browser test covers those interactions. The RC starter lesson now computes τ = RC, explains the 63% point at one time constant, and guides the learner to double R1; it shows a current-value prediction while old results are stale and compares the 10 ms prediction with the solved Firefox ngspice result after rerun. No WASM rebuild was required. The LED current-limiter lesson now uses ngspice’s solved forward drop to estimate R1 current, guides the learner from 330 Ω to 1 kΩ, shows the updated estimate while results are stale, and compares it after rerunning in Firefox. The current-source lesson now applies V = I × R to the live source/load values, guides a change from 1 kΩ to 2 kΩ, displays the stale-result prediction, and verifies the resulting 2 V operating point in Firefox. The bridge-rectifier lesson now predicts that increasing C1 from 220 µF to 470 µF reduces ripple to about 46.8% at the same load and frequency; its Firefox ngspice workflow samples capacitor voltage between peaks before and after the edit and verifies the reduction. The voltage-controlled sources lesson now calculates E1 output from the live gain and solved VIN, guides a gain change from 2 to 3, displays a stale-result prediction, and compares both E1 and unchanged G1 outputs after a Firefox ngspice rerun.

A basic voltage-divider example now places two equal 1 kΩ resistors across 10 V, starts at an operating-point analysis, and selects VIN/VOUT. Its guided card explains the 5 V midpoint and invites changing one resistor; netlist and pinned-WASM operating-point regressions check the expected result, and a focused browser workflow verifies the solved midpoint.

A parallel-resistor current-divider example now places 1 kΩ and 2 kΩ branches across 10 V and selects VIN, I(R1), and I(R2). Its guided lesson derives 10 mA and 5 mA with I = V/R, compares the solved branch currents, and invites changing R2 to 1 kΩ to make both currents equal. Netlist coverage and Firefox/ngspice verify topology, probes, current animation, stale-result prediction, and the equal-current rerun. The example is searchable and appears in the Basics category; all 55 Firefox UI smoke tests, all 30 unit groups, TypeScript, and the production build pass. No WASM rebuild or Pages run was needed.

The voltage-divider lesson now continues after the first solve with a specific experiment: change the lower resistor R2 from 1 kΩ to 2 kΩ and rerun; the midpoint rises from 5 V to about 6.67 V. The formula `VOUT = VIN × R2 / (R1 + R2)` computes an expected output for the current values, including while old results are marked stale; after rerunning the lesson compares that prediction with ngspice’s solved value and suggests restoring equal resistors to return to half-supply. Its browser workflow follows the learner through the initial result, keyboard part selection, inspector edit, stale-result cue and predicted output, guided rerun, and agreement between prediction and solved value. The focused workflow passes in Chromium and Firefox using the existing local runtime; typecheck, all 28 unit groups, and the standard frontend build pass. The build reports 205.4 KiB initial JavaScript gzip against its 230 KiB budget. No WASM rebuild or hosted run was done.

A guided bridge-rectifier lesson uses four built-in diodes, a 50 Hz source, a load, a reservoir capacitor, and a high-value output reference. The lesson directs the learner to probe voltage across the capacitor so the waveform and charge animation show the full-wave output between peaks. Netlist coverage checks diode orientation, output/load/reference connectivity, and transient settings; a focused Chromium run with the existing local engine verifies the capacitor stays above 3 V at the 10 ms between-peak sample and exposes the charge readout.

The transient measurement editor offers labeled node-voltage and common R/C/L/voltage-source/current-source vectors in accessible pickers, while keeping editable SPICE expressions for advanced vectors. A browser regression checks node/current choices, free-form expression synchronization, and saved project state.

The guided inverting-amplifier lesson reuses the original five-pin educational op-amp IC to teach negative feedback. A 0.5 V input, 10 kΩ input resistor, and 20 kΩ feedback resistor produce approximately −1 V; the non-inverting input is grounded and the feedback node is exposed for probing. The domain test checks pin order and feedback topology, and a focused browser run verifies the solved output (−0.99997 V) using the existing local WASM runtime; no engine rebuild is required.

The guided “Inductor current ramp” lesson fills the standalone RL transient gap between RC charging and RLC ringing. A 5 V pulse drives a 100 Ω resistor and 100 mH inductor (1 ms time constant); its default plots show input voltage, inductor voltage, and inductor current. The lesson explains the gradual current rise and decay after switch-off. After the first solve, it asks the learner to double R1 and predict the result: the time constant halves to 0.5 ms and current approaches 25 mA instead of 50 mA. While the edited project has stale results, the lesson shows the current R1 and L1 values and offers a direct rerun button. After the second solve, it derives time constant and steady current from both edited values and suggests changing L1; changing L1 and rerunning then shows how inductance changes ramp speed without changing the final current. The focused browser regression uses the existing local runtime to verify the initial rise/decay, both stale-result states and guided reruns, and the time-domain effect of changing L1; the domain test verifies the original netlist and selected traces. No WASM rebuild is needed.

RL time-constant prediction (2026-10-14): Before the first run, the inductor-ramp lesson now asks the learner to estimate τ = L1/R1 and explains that an ideal step response reaches about 63% of final current after one time constant. Its prediction recalculates from the edited component values and clears the previous answer when either value changes. A focused Firefox regression checks a wrong and correct 1 ms prediction, the updated 0.5 ms estimate after doubling R1, and that Run remains available. All 34 domain-test groups, TypeScript, and the production build pass; the local ngspice runtime was not rebuilt. M1.5 remains active for broader first-use and guided-example review.

Supply-droop prediction (2026-10-14): The internal-resistance lesson now asks learners to predict VOUT from V1, RSOURCE, and RLOAD before simulation, then explains the loaded-divider equation and why heavier loads increase voltage sag. Changing any of the three values clears the answer and recalculates it; Firefox verifies the default 4.545 V estimate and the 2.5 V result after reducing RLOAD from 1 kΩ to 100 Ω. The 34 domain-test groups, TypeScript, and production build pass; the initial JS+CSS transfer is 219.7 KiB gzip against the 245 KiB budget. No ngspice rebuild or Pages workflow ran. M1.5 remains active for broader first-use and guided-example review.

Zener-current prediction (2026-10-14): Before simulation, the Zener-regulator lesson now asks learners to estimate current through R1 using `(V1 − 5.1 V)/R1`. Feedback identifies this as a first-order estimate, notes the small output-reference branch current, and distinguishes it from ngspice's nonlinear Zener model. Changing V1 or R1 resets the response and updates the estimate; Firefox checks 6.9 mA at 12 V / 1 kΩ and 3.45 mA after doubling R1. All 34 domain-test groups, TypeScript, and the production build pass; initial JS+CSS is 219.7 KiB gzip against the 245 KiB budget. No ngspice rebuild or Pages workflow ran. M1.5 remains active for broader first-use and guided-example review.

Flyback protection lesson (2026-10-12): The pulse-driven 10 Ω / 100 mH coil and antiparallel diode now use ten explicit source, series, clamp, and ground-return wires. DRIVE/COIL labels remain only as probes/references. Firefox/ngspice verifies the negative coil-voltage clamp, nonzero diode current, and correctly directed wire animation at 15 ms after the source switches off; a netlist regression protects pin order and topology. No WASM rebuild is needed.

Probe-menu clarity (2026-10-12): Right-click voltage choices on generic two-terminal components say “terminal 1” and “terminal 2” instead of exposing the internal `pin1`/`pin2` identifiers. Named pins such as source positive/negative and transformer winding terminals retain their electrical names. The focused Firefox probe workflow verifies the terminal wording and a solved component-voltage trace.

Flyback lesson follow-up (2026-10-12): After a solved transient, the guided card now reads the actual V(COIL) and I(D1) samples nearest 15 ms, labels changed-run values stale, and connects the observed current decay to the approximate L/R time constant. It proposes doubling R1 as a hands-on decay experiment. Focused Firefox/ngspice coverage checks both solved values and the next-step guidance using the existing local runtime; no WASM rebuild.

Parallel LED lesson (2026-10-12): Added two separately current-limited LED branches from one 5 V rail.
